# 2024 LatAm Retail & eCommerce Outlook: Turning the Corner, Slowly

Into 2024, we see a mixed outlook for consumption, but expect accelerating growth for eCommerce — supported by our AlphaWise survey & industry models. Our OW calls favor digital leaders (MELI, DESP) & discounters (ASAI3, CRFB3, WMX). We downgrade MGLU3 (UW) & Falabella (UW); upgrade Liverpool (EW).

We look for LatAm retail trends to move past the trough as interest rates decrease, and our AlphaWise Consumer survey shows constructive sentiment, though we expect the improvement pace to be gradual. As we highlighted in our AlphaWise Brazil & Mexico Consumer Survey analysis, we see pockets of optimism, with upward inflections in Brazil & Mexico consumers' household financial expectations. An ongoing easing cycle in Brazil, and expected interest rate cuts in Mexico, should provide incremental support for consumption. However, given the Morgan Stanley LatAm Economics team's "Tight for Longer" 2024 call — forecasting Brazil's benchmark at 10.0% for year-end, and Mexico at 8.5% — we believe the pace of a rebound is likely to be muted. Our Brazil Retail Leading Indicator (BReLI) points to real retail sales growth of +1.0% for 2024E, decelerating versus the +2.0% tracking for 2023E. Our economists' forecasts for decelerating employment and real wage growth, coupled with higher-for-longer Brazil benchmark rates, weighs on our model, and we see the output as consistent with our fundamental call against a more substantial retail sales pickup in '24.

For online retail, we look for GMV to reaccelerate to +14% y/y in USD; while our AlphaWise eCommerce data shows traction for international platforms, we do not expect broad competitive disruption. Within LatAm eCommerce, we forecast industry gross merchandise value (GMV) growing +14% y/y in USD, with penetration increasing +130bps y/y (reaching 14.3%). For Brazil, we forecast +16% ex-FX growth, up from just +6% in 2023E, as we lap disruption at Americanas as well as the peak drag from companies' initiatives targeting profitability over growth (i.e., higher take rates, lower subsidies). Our November 2023 AlphaWise Brazil & Mexico eCommerce survey, detailed in this note, shows an up-trend in past week purchases in both countries, along with positive online intent particularly for the apparel and beauty categories. While at the platform level, we highlight survey data traction for international operators. In Brazil, Shopee (owned by Sea Limited, SE.N, covered by Divya Kothiyal) maintained its top position for surveyed purchase frequency, though with limited y/y change, while Amazon (AMZN.N, covered by Brian Nowak) saw a y/y increase. making an online purchase on Temu (owned by PDD Holdings, PDD.O, covered by Eddy Wang); while survey and app download data point to a sharp early ramp, we see barriers preventing widespread disruption. Notably, our survey data showed cross-border platforms ranking below-average for product quality and delivery speeds, with only mixed perceptions on pricing. Particularly for MercadoLibre (MELI.O), while we acknowledge the traction in survey data for Amazon Brazil and the uptake in downloads for Temu Mexico, we have conviction that MELI's marketplace scale leadership, logistics network, and financial services tie-ins combine for a sustainable competitive moat.

Our OW-rated stock calls center on digital platform leaders — MELI (Top Pick) for eCommerce, DESP for online travel — and on food retail discounters Assai / Carrefour Brasil / Walmex. We broadly see our calls aligning with Morgan Stanley LatAm Equity Strategist Nikolaj Lippmann and team's bias toward secular growth across LatAm, and lower beta in Brazil. For MELI, we see accelerated share gains from '23 further cementing MercadoLibre's leading marketplace position into '24 — with our global deep dive pointing to pricing power in areas such as advertising and logistics. We see multi-pronged profitability drivers, and look for '24E net margins +320bps y/y to 11.0%; we see the shares at 42x '24 P/E, for our outsized +80% 2022-2025E EPS CAGR. While for Despegar (DESP.N), we see continued room to run for our August 2023 assumption of coverage thesis around secular plus cyclical support within LatAm online travel bookings; we look for improved FCF conversion to further drive valuation support in '24. Within Food Retail, our Discounters: Growth Plus Resilience analysis illuminated discount food retail as one of the bestpositioned retail formats globally, with growth through macro cycles plus resilience from the online threat. For Brazil cash & carry operators Assai (ASAI3.SA) and Carrefour Brasil (CRFB3.SA), we look for '23 headwinds to turn to tailwinds for the year ahead, with a move past food deflation, sales/margin uplifts from converted stores, and financial expense relief amid lower interest rates. For Walmex (WALMEX.MX), our comparative analysis with Femsa (FMX.N, covered by Ricardo Alves) supports our conviction in the company's earnings compounding and high return on capital model.

We downgrade Magazine Luiza to Underweight, on durable goods caution and limited survey traction in newer categories. For department stores, amid diverging earnings revision trends we downgrade Falabella to Underweight, and upgrade Liverpool to Equal-weight. For Magazine Luiza (MGLU3.SA), we downgrade to UW as our survey data showed limited traction outside of core electronics/appliances, weighing on marketplace optionality. In a year where we expect a muted rebound for consumption — impacting sales of higher-ticket durable categories (i.e., appliances, electronics, furniture) — we see risk that Magalu's earnings recovery gets pushed out. With near-breakeven net income for 2024E and a 31x P/E multiple on our 2025E earnings, we see limited valuation support. Within the department store channel for LatAm, we continue to see structural traffic and margin headwinds from the shift to online, while this thesis has been slow to play out particularly in Mexico. As diverging earnings trends have not been fully reflected in the stocks, we swap ratings — downgrading Chile-based Falabella (FALABELLA.SN) to Underweight and upgrading Mexico-based Liverpool (LIVEPOLC1.MX) to Equal-weight. With the magnitude of Falabella's EBITDA

contraction upon lapping Chile stimulus, we see premium trading multiples (31x 2024E P/E and 18x 2025E) even when incorporating a sales/margin rebound for the year ahead. While Liverpool has posted steadier operating trends and now trades at an undemanding 8x 2024E P/E, supporting a more balanced risk-reward setup. For Magazine Luiza, we see -8% PT downside, with a 0.9:1.0 bull-bear skew; for Falabella, -22% PT downside, with a 0.2:1.0 bull-bear skew. Our rating and price target changes are summarized in Exhibit 1 .

Where could we be wrong? A greater ramp in eCommerce competition could weigh on the industry profit pool, and a sharper easing cycle could render our relative positioning too conservative. Our survey results show that Brazil and Mexico eCommerce remain competitive markets, with both existing local/ international platforms and newer entrants such as Temu. If subsidies and discounts re-ramp and drive a broader industry response, this could weigh on margins for our eCommerce-exposed companies. While from a cyclical perspective, the discretionary retailers in our coverage, particularly in Brazil, face interest rate sensitivity across cyclical goods sales, financial expenses, and cost of capital — driving upside risk in a scenario where rate cuts accelerate, and downside against a higher-for-longer interest rate backdrop. Our ratings skew favors digital platform leaders and food retail discounters over discretionary retail operators, while amid a sharper easing cycle our discretionary retail views could prove too conservative.

Read on for: Our 3 Key '24 Debates, 2023 AlphaWise eCommerce Survey, Brazil Retail Leading Indicator, LatAm eCommerce industry model, and stock upgrade/ downgrade detail. We have also refreshed our sub-sector, company, and Risk-Reward views for 2024.

## Executive Summary

Into 2024, we see a mixed outlook for consumption, but expect accelerating growth for eCommerce — supported by our AlphaWise survey and industry models. Our investment framework favors companies and sub-sectors where we have conviction around the path to profitable growth. Into 2024, we look for LatAm retail trends to move past the trough as interest rates decrease, and our AlphaWise Consumer survey shows constructive sentiment. However, amid our economists' "tight for longer" interest rates call, we expect the improvement pace to be gradual. For LatAm eCommerce, we look for accelerating trends, with a more normalized base — as we lap disruption at Americanas as well as the peak drag from companies' initiatives targeting profitability over growth. Our OW stock calls favor digital leaders (MELI, DESP) and food retail discounters (ASAI3, CRFB3, WMX) — keeping with our relative preference for areas of the market where we see profitable growth visibility. We downgrade Magazine Luiza to Underweight, on durable goods caution and limited survey traction in newer categories. For department stores, amid diverging earnings revision and trading multiple trends we swap ratings: downgrading Falabella to Underweight, and upgrading Liverpool to Equal-weight. We remain UW-rated on Casas Bahia (BHIA3.SA), with electronics headwinds and pressured net income flow-through, and on C&A Brasil (CEAB3.SA), with downside to our price target as improving execution in Brazil apparel appears more than fully reflected in the shares.

Our three key debates for 2024 center on LatAm consumer conditions, eCommerce growth drivers, and international competition:

## Debate #1: Will the LatAm Consumer Pick Up or Muddle Through in '24?

We look for LatAm retail trends to move past the trough as interest rates decrease, and our AlphaWise Consumer survey shows constructive sentiment, though we expect the improvement pace to be gradual. Ongoing easing cycles in Brazil and Chile ( Exhibit 3 ), and expected interest rate cuts in Mexico, could provide some incremental support for consumption. However, the Morgan Stanley LatAm Economics team (led by Fernando Sedano) sees the 2024 outlook as Tight for Longer, with various factors weighing on growth in LatAm. For sentiment, as highlighted in our AlphaWise Brazil & Mexico Consumer Survey analysis we see pockets of optimism, with upward inflections in Brazil & Mexico consumers' household financial expectations ( Exhibit 4 ). Incorporating factors including benchmark interest rates and consumer confidence, our Brazil Retail Leading Indicator (BReLI) points to real retail sales growth of +1.0% for 2024E, decelerating versus the +2.0% tracking for 2023E ( Exhibit 5 ). Put together, we believe LatAm retail has moved past peak periods of macro- and pandemic-driven disruption. While when considering still-high rates through the year (our economists forecast Brazil's benchmark at 10.0% for year-end, and Mexico at 8.5%), we believe the pace of a rebound — particularly for higher-ticket durable categories (i.e., appliances, electronics, furniture) — is likely to be muted. Our economists' forecasts for decelerating employment and real wage growth, coupled with higher-for-longer Brazil benchmark rates, weigh on our BReLI model, and we see the output as consistent with our fundamental call against a more substantial retail sales acceleration in '24.

## Debate #2: Can LatAm eCommerce Have a "Normalized" Growth Year?

For online retail, we look for GMV to reaccelerate to +14% y/y in USD, with a cleaner growth setup as we move past peak pandemic impacts, Americanas disruption, and companies' profitability-over-growth shift. Within LatAm eCommerce, we forecast industry gross merchandise value (GMV) growing +14% y/y in USD, up from +8% for 2023E, with penetration increasing +130bps y/y (reaching 14.3%); see Exhibit 6 . By country, we forecast '24E ex-FX eCommerce growth of +16% for Brazil, +18% for Mexico, +187% for Argentina, +13% for Chile, and +23% for Colombia. Our Brazil forecast notably marks a pickup from just +6% growth in 2023E, as we lap disruption at Americanas as well as the peak drag from companies' initiatives targeting profitability over growth (i.e., higher take rates, lower subsidies). Our 6th AlphaWise Brazil & Mexico eCommerce survey, conducted in November 2023 and with results new in this note, shows an uptrend in past week purchases in both countries ( Exhibit 7 for Brazil), along with positive online intent particularly for the apparel and beauty categories ( Exhibit 8 ). The survey covers \~2,500 consumers in Brazil and Mexico, following up on 2022, 2021, 2020, 2019, and 2017 surveys. Low prices remain the top driver for online purchases in both Brazil and Mexico, but to a decreasing magnitude; more consumers cited the importance of product quality in Brazil, and the factor was the second most-important in Mexico.

## Debate #3: Will International Platforms Disrupt LatAm eCommerce?

While our AlphaWise eCommerce data shows traction for international platforms, we do not expect broad competitive disruption. We asked consumers to rank the top three online retailers from which they purchase most frequently; results over time are shown in Exhibit 9 for Brazil and Exhibit 10 for Mexico. In Brazil, Shopee maintained its top position for surveyed purchase frequency, though with limited y/y change, while Amazon Brazil saw a y/y increase. Among local platforms, in Brazil we highlight the step-change downward for Americanas, and slight declines for Magalu and Casas Bahia; while in Mexico, Walmex saw an upward inflection. Looking to Temu, after \~6 months in Mexico \~10% of consumers in the country reported making an online purchase on the platform — for early traction, though still below the top-3 Mexico platforms (averaging \~45%). Notably, our survey data showed cross-border platforms ranking below-average for product quality and delivery speeds, with only mixed perceptions on pricing (see Exhibit 11 for Mexico). Further detail on attributes and category-specific strengths by platform is included in this note. For MELI, the platform was in second place for surveyed purchase frequency in both countries, and with favorable views on a range of perception metrics. We have conviction that MercadoLibre's logistics network and financial services tie-ins, along with the company's wide lead in LatAm eCommerce GMV scale ( Exhibit 12 ), combine for a sustainable competitive moat.

## 2024 Sub-Sector and Stock Call Overviews

LatAm eCommerce — MercadoLibre (MELI), Magazine Luiza (MGLU3.SA), Casas Bahia (BHIA3.SA), Meliuz (CASH3.SA), Americanas (AMER3.SA): We see divergent trends continuing among the scaled LatAm eCommerce operators in our coverage. OW-rated MELI emerged as the top incremental share gainer from Americanas' disruption, and we see marketplace scale leadership supporting continued take rate monetization and margin expansion. While for Magalu (downgrading to UW) and Casas Bahia (UW), we expect pressured earnings flow-through to persist, amid a slow electronics recovery and limited traction in newer categories. For CASH3 (EW), capital return provides downside support, but visibility around core cash-back operations remains low, while for AMER3 (N-R) we remain Not-Rated amid the ongoing judicial recovery. Key calls: OW MELI; UW MGLU3, BHIA3.

Brazil Apparel Retail — Lojas Renner (LREN3.SA), C&A Modas (CEAB3.SA): We continue to lean cautious on the potential for digitalization investments and competitive pricing pressure to weigh on margins within Brazil apparel, while online apparel survey intent was favorable and we believe the category could benefit from improving consumption trends. For Renner (EW) we see balanced risk-reward, with a trading multiple discount to history, but limited catalyst for upward revisions. For C&A (UW), execution on the sales/margin recovery exceeded our expectations in recent quarters, but this appears overly reflected in the stock following '23 share price appreciation, keeping us Underweight-rated. Key call: UW CEAB3.

Brazil Food Retail — Assai (ASAI3.SA), CBD (PCAR3.SA), Carrefour Brasil (CRFB3.SA): While Brazil cash & carry food retail faced top- to bottom-line pressure in 2023, we retain our structural bullish view on the segment, and look for tactical headwinds to reverse. With '24 support from a move past peak food deflation, sales/margin uplifts from converted stores, and financial expense relief amid lower interest rates, we remain positive on ASAI3 (OW) and CRFB3 (OW). For PCAR3 (EW), we favor the structural simplification efforts, but amid pressured net income flow-through we remain EW-rated. Key calls: OW ASAI3, CRFB3.

LatAm Specialty Retail — Natura & Co (NTCO3.SA), Petz (PETZ3.SA), Mobly (MBLY3.SA), Despegar (DESP.N): Our preferred specialty play is DESP (OW), with support from online travel share gain and profitability momentum which does not appear priced in. Distinct from discretionary retail spending (i.e., electronics, furniture), where '20/'21 stock-up consumption has driven a demand overhang, we see room for travel to benefit from a continued recovery in '24. While across the remaining sector-specialist operators in our coverage, we await greater clarity on the Wave 2 Natura/Avon integration for Natura (EW), around the new store sales/margin maturation curve for Petz (EW), and around the return to Brazil online furniture growth for Mobly (EW) in order to revisit our ratings. Key call: OW DESP.

Mexico Retail — Walmart de Mexico (WALMEX.MX), Liverpool (LIVEPOLC1.MX): We continue to see a lower-volatility retail backdrop in Mexico when compared to Brazil & Chile, with remittance flows remaining an area of support. Our stock preference remains Walmex (OW), with a stapleslike compounding, high ROIC model, and with comfort around the ongoing monopolistic practices investigation. For Liverpool (upgrading to EW), our structural caution on department store margins remains, but this appears to be reflected in the share price. Key call: OW WALMEX.

Chile & Other LatAm Retail — Falabella S.A. (FALABELLA.SN), Cencosud (CENCOSUD.SN): Chile retail sales have continued to face pressure upon lapping consumer stimulus measures. Our economics team expects a modest Chile GDP recovery into 2024, but with still-tight monetary policy. Cencosud (EW) remains our relative preference, with food retail end-market exposure and undemanding valuation. While for Falabella (downgrading to UW), coupling stock appreciation in '23 with negative estimate revisions, we now see an unfavorable risk-reward skew. Key call: UW FALABELLA.

# Debate #1: Will the LatAm Consumer Pick Up or Muddle Through in '24?

<table><tr><td>Morgan Stanley View</td><td>Where We Could Be Wrong</td></tr><tr><td>We believe peak headwinds are behind for the LatAm regional consumer, supported by lower inflation and interest rate easing cycles, while our AlphaWise survey illuminates Brazil and Mexico consumers&#x27; constructive expectations. However, the lagged effect of still-high interest rates (entering 2024 at 11.75% in Brazil and 11.25% in Mexico) could weigh on the magnitude of any retail / consumption upswing.</td><td>If inflation resurges and interest rates remain even higher-for-longer, this would represent a key downside risk to consumption and retail sales. This pressure could be felt most directly within sales of higher- ticket, cyclical goods categories. Our economics team further highlights the risk of slightly weaker global growth weighing on growth across LatAm.</td></tr></table>

## LatAm Retail Top-Down Trends and Outlook

Across major LatAm markets, 2023 saw a continued easing of inflation, driving a setup for interest rate cuts throughout 2024. After increasing throughout 2021 and through mid-2022 across Brazil, Mexico, and Chile, inflationary pressures eased across all three countries in 2023 ( Exhibit 19 ). The Morgan Stanley Macroeconomics team (led by Fernando Sedano) looks for year-end 2024E inflation of 4.2% in Brazil (4.6% for 2023), 4.0% in Mexico (4.7% for 2023), and 3.0% in Chile (3.9% for 2023), marking the continuation of a disinflationary trend. Alongside easing inflation our economics team sees 2024 as a year of easing benchmark interest rates; Chile saw its first interest rate cut in July 2023, with Brazil in August 2023, and our economists expect the first Mexico rate cut in March 2024 ( Exhibit 20 ). By year-end 2024, their call for 10% rates in Brazil implies - 375bps of cumulative easing, with Mexico rates at 8.5% (-275bps), and Chile at 5.5% (- 575bps). Despite the interest rate reductions, Fernando and team's fundamental call is that high rates for longer and slightly weaker global growth will weigh on growth across LatAm. They believe central banks may need to be cautious to avoid currency volatility, which could be exacerbated by fiscal slippage; for details, see 2024 Latin America Economics Outlook: Tight for Longer (12 Nov 2023).

Exhibit 19: After peaking in mid-2022, inflation has decreased across major LatAm markets, and our economists look for disinflation to continue in 2024
![](images/f3001ded934bba676b40c1415e82602e7b33dee84eb15fb61e64914a86ccbcbf.jpg)
Source: Refinitiv Eikon, Morgan Stanley LatAm Economics estimates

Exhibit 20: … driving support for interest rate easing cycles across Brazil, Mexico, and Chile
![](images/5528099caf0a96ada3753021fd23e52ce08f1310bde7f79072b4b3bf7f534969.jpg)
Source: Refinitiv Eikon, Morgan Stanley LatAm Economics estimates

Amid easing inflation, nominal retail sales in Brazil posted a decelerating trend throughout 2023, while real retail sales showed a slight pickup; trends have been steadier in Mexico. In Exhibit 21 , we show the monthly evolution of Brazil retail sales, broken up into key categories. Overall, retail sales grew +4% y/y in 10M23 in nominal terms, well below the +14% growth in 2022 and +15% in 2021. However, the slowdown was largely due to easing inflation; in real terms, 10M23 growth of +1.6% was above +1.1% in 2022, though below the +2.7% rate from 2021. By category, food and beverages have outgrown the average (+9% nominal growth through 10M22), while the electronics, appliances, and furniture vertical has lagged (at just +0.4%). While category variances remain, we note a move past peak volatility driven by pandemic store closures, stimulus measures, and the associated comparison base. We contrast the Brazil results with Mexico, where we have seen more consistent trends throughout the past few years. As shown in Exhibit 22 , overall Mexico retail sales were up +9% y/y on average in 11M23. While this marked a deceleration from +13% in 2022 and +15% in 2021, there was less of a growth step-change than in Brazil.

Exhibit 21: In Brazil, retail sales grew +4% in nominal terms for 10M23, amid decelerating inflation; food led, while hard goods lagged
![](images/f8eace9ff429b87ba6e2b93b30bdac56364d15a6f8b83a419c13b11600f0f06c.jpg)
Source: IBGE, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 22: In Mexico, nominal growth was +9% y/y through 11M23, for a lesser rate of deceleration when compared to Brazil
![](images/87e88f4f66bb9005b15bde7dc40821cab83edd86430e1d23aa32f30d48d9afd8.jpg)
Source: ANTAD, Morgan Stanley Research

Looking forward, BReLI points to continued real growth, albeit at a decelerating pace; our model output implies real retail sales growth of +1.0% for 2024E, decelerating from the +2.0% tracking for 2023E. As a tool to gauge the outlook for the Brazil consumer, we look to our Brazil Retail Leading Indicator (BReLI) — a linear regression with five variables that show a high correlation to the IBGE official Retail Sales index. With the methodology detailed in our 2020 BReLI note, these model factors include employment growth, real wage growth, interest rate (SELIC), foreign exchange rate (BRL/USD) variation, and consumer confidence growth. Alongside our update in Latin America Consumer: Pockets of Consumer Optimism, but Staying Selective (14 Dec 2023), we have adjusted our model to calculate the variable coefficients excluding the period between March '20 and September '22, essentially excluding peak pandemic distortions — such as store closures, government aid, and the impact of lapping these items. Retail sales outperformed BReLI’s regression by \~20pp from May '20 to May '21, impacted by emergency aid boosts (not captured in our model), before reverting to \~16pp of underperformance for the period of June '21-Dec '22. While retail sales continued to lag BReLI in 1H23, by -4.5pp on average, we have seen the gap gradually narrow in 3Q23, with

IBGE retail sales outperforming BReLI by +0.1pp in September '23 ( Exhibit 23 ). Into 2024, the model forecast for continued positive real retail sales growth, albeit at a sequentially lower pace, is driven by our economists' forecasts for decelerating employment and real wage growth, coupled with higher-for-longer SELIC levels. The resulting output is a blended 2024E real retail sales growth rate of +1.0%, vs. +2.0% in 2023E. A summary of model input factors and retail sales growth trends is included as Exhibit 24 .

Exhibit 23: As we move to a more normalized retail backdrop in 2024, BReLI points to sequentially decelerating real retail sales growth
MS Brazil Retail Leading Indicator (BReLI) - y/y growth

Outside of Brazil, our economists see supportive data points in Mexico (including remittances), while they look for a modest recovery in Chile and expect growth to slow down further in Colombia. In Mexico, our economics team believes GDP growth should remain resilient, with tight labor markets, strong remittances, and rising government transfers driving consumption. They look for continued resilience in remittance flows ( Exhibit 25 ), and foresee real GDP growth of 3.4% in 2023 and then at 2.3% in 2024 amid a US soft landing, most likely impacting Mexico in 2H24 ( Exhibit 26 ). For Chile, '20/'21 retail sales were supported by the liquidity boost driven by pension fund withdrawals, and the overhang from lapping this stimulus continued throughout 2023, with negative real growth ( Exhibit 27 ). For '24, our economics team expects real GDP growth to return positive at +1.9% ( Exhibit 28 ), with a modest recovery on the back of still-tight policies. While they believe idiosyncratic risks, such as around constitutional reform, should be less relevant going forward. For Colombia, expectations are for growth to slow down further on the back of lower domestic demand, with fiscal uncertainty also weighing on the outlook. Our economists look for Colombia to deliver the region's slowest disinflation ( Exhibit 29 ), with only a modest y/y increase in real GDP growth to +1.7% for 2024E ( Exhibit 30 ).

![](images/7a6fd49812c0d9a8c3cc339cde835de3644605b397c1e457b084fab8c5805873.jpg)

## AlphaWise Brazil & Mexico Consumer Survey Highlights

As detailed in Pockets of Consumer Optimism, but Staying Selective (14 Dec 2023), our AlphaWise Consumer Survey showed notable upward inflections in Brazil & Mexico consumers' household financial expectations. In our 12th AlphaWise Brazil and Mexico Survey, conducted October 2023, consumer optimism (despite still-high interest rates in the region) was an area of positive surprise. As shown in Exhibit 31 , as of our survey date 73% of Brazil consumers expect their household financial situations to improve over the next 6 months, inflecting a sizable +20pp upward versus our prior wave, and marking the highest point in our data set. Similarly in Mexico, as shown in Exhibit 32 , 72% of consumers look for improving household financial situations. The constructive net sentiment comes alongside lower rates of concern around inflation, reversing a '22 step-up in inflation-related concerns among Brazil consumers ( Exhibit 33 ). We further see consumers having become less concerned about job loss, debt repayment, and Covid spread, while geopolitical conflicts were an area of increased concern.

Behind the incremental optimism, our survey shows Brazil and Mexico consumers with improving employment trends, confidence around their ability to pay bills, and less "significant impact" from inflation. Among supportive areas for the consumer backdrop per our survey, 53% of respondents in Brazil reported being employed full-time in October 2023, up from 44% in our August 2022 survey ( Exhibit 34 ) and marking a high in our time series. Only 5% reported being unemployed or furloughed, down from 6% last year. Similarly, only 10% of consumers in Brazil cited an inability to pay their bills in full for the current month ( Exhibit 35 ). This marked a +5pp improvement from our last survey, and is favorable to the \~20% average throughout our 2020 and 2021 survey waves. While for inflation, 36% of consumers in Brazil noted a "significant impact" from inflation on their level of spending/consumption over the past few months ( Exhibit 36 ). Concern rates inflected downward by -17pp in our October 2023 survey when compared to the August 2022 wave, and down -27pp from our November 2021 survey. Looking to Mexico ( Exhibit 37 ), 27% of consumers reported a significant impact from inflation, reversing from an increase to 33% in 2022, and coming back in-line with the 2021 concern rate.

# Debate #2: Can LatAm eCommerce Have a 'Normalized' Growth Year?

## Morgan Stanley View

Moving past pandemic tailwinds / re-opening headwinds, companies' profitability-over-growth shift, and the disruptive effects of Americanas' restructuring, we see a cleaner setup for LatAm eCommerce growth in the year ahead. We forecast +28% ex-FX / +14% USD growth for LatAm eCommerce in 2024E, including +16% ex-FX growth in Brazil. AlphaWise data supports our thesis around gains in higher-frequency categories.

## Where We Could Be Wrong

To the downside, persistently high interest rates in the region could weigh on companies' ability to make investments that support eCommerce growth, while further weighing on consumer purchasing power within higher-ticket verticals. While to the upside, in an environment of lower rates plus a depressed comparison base, our forecast for +130bps of penetration gains could ultimately prove conservative.

## LatAm eCommerce Model

We forecast LatAm eCommerce growing +14.1% y/y in USD and +28.3% ex-FX in 2024E, with penetration gains of +130bps y/y (reaching 14.3%). Following a two-year surge from 6.1% penetration in 2019 to 12.1% in 2021, LatAm eCommerce saw a more modest increase to 12.4% penetration in 2022; we look for 2023 to end with 13.0% penetration. While the past two years have represented more modest gains, LatAm has outperformed other major regions, supported by a still-low starting point and structural improvements around logistics, assortment, and service levels. Headwinds have been concentrated primarily within high-penetration, cyclical categories (e.g., electronics) and in the countries with the most difficult stimulus comparisons (e.g., Chile). We have seen a further drag on growth from company-specific events (such as the ongoing Americanas restructuring) and from a broader focus on profitability (with higher take rates and lower subsidies). Looking ahead, we forecast penetration gains of +130bps y/y in 2024E, reaching 14.3%; for comparison, the average increase between 2019 and 2023E was \~170bps. For growth rates, we look for +14.1% y/y in USD terms and +28.3% ex-FX; between 2022 and 2027E, we forecast a +10.4% CAGR in USD, and +20.9% in ex-FX terms. Our regional eCommerce build is summarized as Exhibit 38 .

Exhibit 38: We forecast LatAm eCommerce growing at a +10% USD CAGR between 2022 and 2027E, including +14% growth (+28% ex-FX) for 2024E
LatAm eCommerce Size by Country (US\$bn)
![](images/7052cb38ec749ec145c8e042d1a54b653228949e52d98e6e0a6cd02774b6b29c.jpg)
Source: Euromonitor, Morgan Stanley Research estimate

For Brazil specifically, we forecast +15.8% 2024E growth in BRL, with penetration increasing +150bps y/y to 16.6%, as the industry moves past peak Americanas disruption. 2023 was a year of highly divergent trends for top Brazil eCommerce platforms. Amid the ongoing restructuring at Americanas (see What We Learned from Americanas 2022 Results and Where is Americanas' Share Going?), we estimate GMV at the platform decreased \~70% y/y in 2023, marking a \~10pp headwind to 2023 industry growth. While part of the sales were re-captured, we believe a portion of Americanas volumes were unprofitable sales that were therefore not recovered by the market. On the other side, MercadoLibre GMV reaccelerated, and we look for +27% growth for MELI in 2023. For the year ahead, we see a more normalized base for the Brazil eCommerce industry. As such, we look for +15.8% growth in 2024E, with +150bps of penetration gain (to 16.6%). Our growth forecast compares to the 2019-2023E CAGR of +30.7%, while marking a pickup for our estimate of +6.3% growth in the disrupted 2023 year. Looking through 2027E, we forecast a +11.8% CAGR, with the market size increasing \~1.7x versus 2022 levels, to \~R\$520 billion ( Exhibit 39 ); we look for broad-based penetration gains to resume, most notably in the lower-penetration beauty/health/pet and food/beverage categories ( Exhibit 40 ). Complementary to our category model, in Exhibit 41 we break down Brazil eCommerce growth drivers by shopper count, basket size, and purchase frequency. While market expansion in the '14-'21 period was driven primarily by online shopper growth (at a +13% CAGR), we believe forward growth will be more driven by higher purchase frequencies, consistent with our category expansion thesis.

We see LatAm as a key component of our global emerging markets growth call, as detailed in Global eCommerce: The Power of Marketplaces (23 Apr 2023). For '24E, we forecast ex-FX growth of +15.8% for Brazil eCommerce, with +18.4% growth for Mexico, +187% for Argentina, +13.4% for Chile, and +22.5% for Colombia. This totals +28.3% local currency growth, translating to +14.1% in USD when considering FX depreciation in-line with forward curves. On a 5-year basis ('22-'27E), our LatAm CAGR of +10.4% is above the global average of +8% ( Exhibit 42 ). Despite outsized growth, our forecast 2027E LatAm penetration of 17.7% is slightly lower than the global penetration for 2020 (at 19.3%). LatAm is a key component of our call for a more outsized growth opportunity in less-

penetrated emerging markets ( Exhibit 43 ). Across global eCommerce, we see scaled marketplaces — platforms matching sellers and buyers — as well positioned for profitable growth despite macro volatility. Our proprietary take rate monetization analysis shows greater gains for larger platforms — demonstrating that marketplace scale leadership can drive pricing power and unlock additional service opportunities.

## AlphaWise Evidence – Consumer Behaviors

AlphaWise survey data points to online spend remaining resilient, even in a backdrop where consumers are returning to in-store shopping at above pre-pandemic rates. Calling back to our October 2023 AlphaWise Survey (Pockets of Consumer Optimism, but Staying Selective (14 Dec 2023)), we look to the proportion of Brazil & Mexico consumers who have shopped online over the past month ( Exhibit 44 ). AlphaWise data shows that participation for both grocery and non-grocery buying has been largely stable through our survey time series, though with an uptick versus 2022 levels. Most notably, 64% of Brazil consumers reported buying non-grocery items online over the prior month (as of Oct '23), up from 59% as of Aug '22. We view this resilience as constructive for structural online gains, with greater eCommerce behavior amid a backdrop where visits to the mall continue to normalize, and even exceed pre-pandemic levels. As shown in Exhibit 45 , 55% of consumers in Brazil reported visiting a shopping mall over the past month, up from 51% in our August 2022 survey and marking a new high in our time series. In Mexico, participation was higher (at 74%), though largely unchanged y/y, having normalized at or slightly above the pre-pandemic baseline since mid-2021.

Exhibit 44: We see Brazil and Mexico consumers modestly increasing their online purchase behaviors y/y...

Looking out the next 3 months, consumers expect to increase their shopping activities both online and in-store; Brazil leads Mexico for online intent, with groceries as a standout. We track the net spreads between consumers who expect to perform more versus less of an activity over the next 3 months, with online shopping in Exhibit 46 and mall visits in Exhibit 47 . The positive net spreads of more vs. less for both sets of activities aligns with top-down survey data around household financial situation optimism. For online shopping behavior in particular, we note more positive trends in Brazil than Mexico. In Brazil, a net 48% of consumers expect to buy more grocery items online, up +10pp from our prior survey wave and marking a high in our data set. For non-grocery items, net intent was 38%, up from 35% last year. In Mexico, the net intent is lower, at 25% for non-grocery and 22% for grocery. Looking to mall visits, net expectations remained in positive territory, with similar results versus our prior survey wave for both Brazil and Mexico.

## AlphaWise Evidence — eCommerce Trends

Survey data supports our view around the continued shift toward buying online; pastweek purchases increased y/y in Brazil, and reached new highs in Mexico. For a more detailed dive into eCommerce-specific drivers, we look to the 6th edition of our AlphaWise Brazil & Mexico eCommerce survey, with data new to this note. In Exhibit 48 and Exhibit 49 , we show survey data for when consumers most recently bought online, for Brazil and Mexico, respectively. Forty-six percent of consumers in Brazil reported making an online purchase in the past week; while this marked a reversion from 51% in 2021, the result was an increase from 44% in 2022, and remained above pre-2021 levels. Only 10% of Brazil consumers have not made an online purchase within the last 6 months, down from 13% in 2022. In Mexico, past-week purchase penetration was 47%, marking a notable upward inflection from 39% in 2022, representing a new high in our survey time series. Only 10% of Mexico consumers have not made an online purchase within the past 6 months, also down notably from 15% in 2022.

levels in Brazil, while increasing from '22

Low prices remain the top driver for online purchases in both Brazil and Mexico, but to a decreasing magnitude; more consumer cited the importance of product quality in Brazil, and the factor was the second most-important in Mexico. As shown in Exhibit 50 and Exhibit 51 , low prices are the top consideration for online shopping in both Brazil and Mexico. While in Brazil the importance of low prices decreased y/y, reversing from a 2022 increase, and in Mexico there was a continued modest decrease in reported importance of low prices. Conversely, Brazil saw a new high in consumers citing the importance of "quality of products available" (at 29%). In Mexico, quality was cited as a top-three factor by a similar 26% of consumers, overtaking free shipping as the secondmost important purchase driver. In both countries, logistics-related items (free shipping and fast delivery) rounded out the top four factors. Conversely, when looking for reasons of not shopping online ( Exhibit 52 and Exhibit 53 ), logistics-related items (such as costs, returns, speeds, and concerns about damages) featured highly. We note that in Brazil, "already buying everything online" was cited by 29% of consumers as the reason for not engaging more with eCommerce; while this was the second-most cited attribute, the concern rate was in-line with 29% in 2022.

For delivery times, trends were largely stable y/y, with 3-5 days as the most prevalent option in both Brazil and Mexico; in both countries, the trend of increasing 1- to 2-day deliveries modestly reverted. Consumers' most common delivery times for online purchases are shown in Exhibit 54 for Brazil and Exhibit 55 for Mexico. In Brazil, 3- to 5- day and 6- to 10-day delivery remain the most-often used delivery options. While 1- to 2- day has seen a multiyear up-trend versus 2022 levels (of 45%), we saw a reversion to 41% in 2023, moving into fourth place. Same-day delivery, in fifth place, similarly saw a slight decrease in prevalence y/y. Looking to Mexico, we see an overall trend of shorter delivery windows than in Brazil, with 3- to 5-day, 1- to 2-day, and same-day among the three most prevalent delivery options. While similar to Brazil, 1- to 2-day delivery penetration of 64% marked a step-back from 72% in 2022. Broadly, we see our covered companies continuing to invest in eCommerce logistics, while survey results point to limited incremental benefit on perceived delivery speeds during 2023.

From a category perspective, apparel and beauty were among the highlights; electronics showed a modest rebound in Brazil, but continued pressure in Mexico. In Exhibit 56 and Exhibit 57 , we show which categories consumers in Brazil and Mexico reported buying online within the past 12 months. Clothing / accessories remained the top category in both countries, at 63% past 12 month purchase behavior in Brazil (61% in 2022) and 62% in Mexico (60% in 2022). Beauty products, athletic apparel, and mobile devices rounded out the top four categories for both countries, in-line with last year. For Brazil, we saw y/y increases for mobile devices (52%, vs. 50% in 2022) and consumer electronics (43%, vs. 39% in 2022), while past 12-month purchases in both categories remained below 2019-21 levels. In Mexico, purchase penetration of mobile devices and consumer electronics slightly decreased y/y, with each marking lows in our data set going back to 2019.

Clothing / shoes and athletic apparel followed for increased purchases, while higher ticket verticals (i.e., appliances / furnishings / electronics) lagged. Looking to the grocery category, the more versus less net spread decreased to 46% in 2023, from 61% in 2022. Conversely in Mexico ( Exhibit 59 ), the groceries and household products category continued to lead for both increases in purchases (66%) and the more versus less net spread (59%). Across all categories surveyed, the "increased versus decreased" spread was positive, averaging +46pp net in Brazil (+50pp in 2022) and +41pp in Mexico (+38pp in 2022). The average across categories was 45% of consumers in Brazil and 46% in Mexico expecting to increase online purchases, versus 12% in Brazil and 13% in Mexico for decreases. ## Omni-Channel & Apps

From a consumer perspective, our AlphaWise survey shows that retail behavior remains omni-channel. Across Brazil ( Exhibit 62 ) and Mexico ( Exhibit 63 ), consumers' path to purchase includes both online and offline shopping behaviors. For checking prices, behavior skews more toward online in Brazil, and with an online/offline balance in Mexico, while browsing and product comparisons are done fairly evenly within both channels. We see online becoming a bigger part of the research process, with y/y increases in both countries for use of reviews / social media and help from online advisors. While purchase behavior for buy online / pickup in-store ranked lower among our surveyed activities, the 2023 results marked follow-through from a step-up in 2022. With consumers continuing to pursue a mix of online and offline behavior, we see survey results continuing to corroborate the need for LatAm retailers to invest behind omni-channel solutions.

Exhibit 62: Consumers' path to purchase includes both online and in-store behaviors…

In Mexico ( Exhibit 65 ), consumers cited a similar 43% of purchases being done on smartphone apps. ## Payments & Retail Financial Services

Card-based methods remain the primary online shopping payment types (credit in Brazil, debit in Mexico), while among alternative methods we saw uptake for PIX in Brazil continue to ramp. Credit cards held their spot as the most common eCommerce payment option in Brazil ( Exhibit 66 ), followed by PIX, an instant payments platform. PIX has risen from <5% in our 2020 survey (soon after the PIX launch), now being cited as a top-three option for 73% of surveyed consumers (60% in 2022). Bank slips ("boletos") appear to be the largest source of share donation, cited by 36% of consumers and down - 39pp from 2020 levels. A declining proportion of consumers cited using mobile / ewallets as well, while we note the potential for some overlap as PIX payments are generally made within a wallet app. Despite similar proportions of Brazil consumers citing credit cards and PIX as top-three payment options, credit cards retained a wide lead as the single most-used payment option, at 57% versus 23% for PIX ( Exhibit 67 ). In Mexico, debit cards were the most-used eCommerce payment option, followed by mobile wallets and credit cards.

Exhibit 66: Credit cards are the top payment mode for online shopping in Brazil, and PIX is rising quickly for prevalence as a top-three option...

For mobile wallet apps, PayPal led for surveyed usage in both Brazil and Mexico, followed by Mercado Pago; other retailers' fintech app platforms lagged. Looking to P12M mobile wallet use in Brazil ( Exhibit 68 ) and Mexico ( Exhibit 69 ), PayPal (covered by James Faucette) leads in both countries, though with a second consecutive y/y decrease in Brazil. MELI's MercadoPago is second in both Brazil and Mexico, with y/y increases in both markets; surveyed usage in Brazil was above PicPay (private) for the first time since 2020. Across the remainder of our coverage, the mobile wallet offerings continue to lag in the survey; MGLU's MagaluPay (part of the main Magalu app), Via's BanQi, and Walmex's Cashi each had <5% P12M usage, with limited incremental y/y traction. Usage for Americanas' Ame at 9% reverted from 18% in 2022, marking a new low in our survey time series. When thinking about which features are most important in a mobile wallet, Brazil consumers most often cited ease of use, ability to pay bills, and peer-to-peer transfers ( Exhibit 70 ); these factors were similar in 2022. In Mexico, the most important features cited by consumers are ease of use, ease of adding funds, and wide acceptance ( Exhibit 71 ).

# Debate #3: Will International Platforms Disrupt LatAm eCommerce?

<table><tr><td>Morgan Stanley View</td><td>Where We Could Be Wrong</td></tr><tr><td>While we acknowledge the traction in survey data for Amazon Brazil and the sharp ramp in downloads for Temu Mexico, we have conviction that MercadoLibre&#x27;s marketplace scale leadership, logistics network, and financial services tie-ins combine for a sustainable competitive moat. We believe international platforms can benefit from a growing LatAm eCommerce TAM, without driving competitive disruption.</td><td>Brazil and Mexico eCommerce are competitive, multi-player markets, including local and international platforms; if cross-border operators re- ramp their discounting and take rate subsidies, this could drive a reversal from the 2022/2023 trend of rationality, potentially pressuring growth for other operators and weighing on the industry profit pool.</td></tr></table>

## AlphaWise Evidence — Platforms In-Focus

In-line with 2022 data, our AlphaWise survey shows Shopee as the most frequent eCommerce purchase destination in Brazil, with Amazon leading in Mexico; MELI ranked second in both countries. We asked consumers to rank the top three online retailers from which they purchase most frequently; results are shown in Exhibit 72 for Brazil and Exhibit 73 for Mexico. In Brazil, Shopee led, cited as a top-three platform by 45% of respondents, with 24% ranking the platform as number one. Shopee was followed by MELI (41%), and Amazon (34%), then with a step-down to Brazil-based platforms Magalu (18%), Americanas (18%), and Casas Bahia (8%). Within Mexico, Amazon led, cited as a top-three platform by 51% of consumers, and number one by 27%. Amazon was followed by MELI (45%) and Walmex (45%), then with a step-down to Mexico department store operator Liverpool (19%). Cross-border platform Temu, which entered Mexico in May 2023, was listed as a top-three platform by 10% of consumers in the country.

Exhibit 72: Shopee was the top-ranked platform for purchase frequency in Brazil, followed by MELI and Amazon…
![](images/8f3f75445439a398a38f47e9a7d1ce1e2f3b3295d3a05746eca8b183ae475392.jpg)
Source: AlphaWise, Morgan Stanley Research
Note: Americanas includes Americanas, Submarino, Shoptime, Soubarato; Cass Bahia includes Casas Bahia, Ponto; Magazine Luiza does not include Netshoes, Zattini.

Exhibit 73: … while in Mexico, Amazon ranked highest for purchase frequency, also followed by MELI

Source: AlphaWise, Morgan Stanley Research Note: Walmex includes Walmart Express, Walmart, Sam's Club, Bodega Aurrera; Liverpool includes Liverpool, Suburbia.

For key trends by platforms, in Brazil we highlight Shopee's stabilization, Amazon's gains, and Americanas' drop; in Mexico, we highlight Walmex's gains and the early emergence of Temu. In Exhibit 74 and Exhibit 75 , we compare the survey results for frequent purchase destination in 2023 versus prior years. Looking to Brazil, Shopee saw the greatest step-changes in 2021 and 2022, but 2023 data show the platform leveling off, with purchase penetration near-flat y/y at 45%. MELI ranked second in Brazil, increasing +4pp y/y to 41%, reversing 2022's decline. Amazon led among gainers, increasing +5pp to 34%, the platform's highest result in our survey time series. Conversely, Americanas declined a sizable -19pp to 18%. While quarterly results have pointed to MELI as a key gainer of Americanas share, we believe our survey data show that Amazon was also a beneficiary. For Brazil-based platforms Magazine Luiza and Casas Bahia, despite the Americanas tailwind our survey showed slight y/y declines. In Mexico, Amazon retained the top spot, followed by MELI, while both showed reversion from 2022 gains. The most notable increase in Mexico was for Walmex, cited as a top-three platform by 45% of consumers, up from 38% in 2022 and 33% in 2021. While Temu registered as a top-three platform by 10% of Mexico consumers, with just half a year in the country at the time of our survey, the result was below that of other major Mexico eCommerce platforms. For another view of purchase trends, in Exhibit 76 (Brazil) and Exhibit 77 (Mexico) we show the proportion of consumers citing an increase versus decrease in spending across platforms over the past 12 months; this data similarly showed relative underperformance of Brazil local operators within the country during 2023.

At the category-level in Brazil, both MELI and Amazon showed breadth, along with y/y gains for electronics; Shopee surveyed stronger for apparel & beauty, and Magalu / Casas Bahia for large appliances. In Exhibit 78 , we show the proportion of survey respondents that bought a certain merchandise category from each operator over the past 12 months; in our view, this illuminates the relative positioning of the Brazil platforms for specific purchase occasions. In Exhibit 79 , we show the y/y change in past 12-month purchases. Among top operators, Shopee had the greatest proportion of consumers who purchased clothing / shoes / accessories (at 48%), and beauty / personal care items (at 38%) on the platform; while on a y/y basis, Shopee trends were minimally-changed. For Amazon, we note y/y gains in consumer electronics and appliances, while MELI's y/y gains were broad-based (excluding apparel). Both Amazon and MELI had category breadth within our survey overall, while Amazon retains outsized strength in books; for home improvement / tools, MELI led among platforms surveyed. At Magazine Luiza and Casas Bahia, we continue to see an over-indexing toward large appliances and electronics, while for Americanas (which had previously surveyed well across categories) there were broad y/y declines.

In books, Amazon retained its category leadership. With a y/y increase in the category, Liverpool over-indexed for clothing, and while the base remained low, clothing was the top major category for Temu as well. Looking to Walmex, the platform maintained a wide lead in groceries, at 75% versus Amazon at 32% and MELI at 19%. Digging in on platform demographics, we see cross-border operators (Shopee, Temu) over-indexing toward younger and lower-income cohorts; in Brazil, we see Amazon over-indexing within Class A and under-indexing for Class C. In Exhibit 82 , we break down the Brazil eCommerce purchase frequency data by age cohort; in Exhibit 83 , by socio-economic class; Exhibit 84 and Exhibit 85 show these trends for Mexico. In Brazil, Shopee continues to lead within the age 18-24 cohort, with 56% ranking the platform as a top-three destination (MELI 34%). While within the 35-44, 45-54, and 55+ cohorts, MELI leads. By socio-economic class, Shopee over-indexes with Class C consumers, cited by 50% (MELI 37%). Distinct from the Brazil trend, MELI over-indexes toward younger consumer cohorts in Mexico, with Amazon more prevalent in older demographics; Amazon overindexes for Class A and Class B consumers as well. For Temu, while the Mexico ramp remains early-stage, we see greater skew toward younger consumers (15% for 18-24 versus 6% for 55+) and lower-income demographics (12% for Class C, vs 9% for B and 10% for A).

We see cross-border operators Shopee and Temu ranking unfavorably for product quality and delivery speeds; while this is balanced by favorable price perception for Shopee Brazil, the Temu Mexico result was more muted. In our survey, we asked around consumers' perception of various attributes of the shopping experience for key platforms; in Exhibit 86 for Brazil and Exhibit 87 for Mexico, we show the perception ratings relative to the average of top platforms. We see the data as favorable for MELI, with above-average perception versus the peer set for each of five key attributes in both Brazil and Mexico. While we believe the data further shows differences in the shopping experience for cross-border platforms versus other operators. In Brazil, we see Shopee ranking favorably for price and offers/promotions, consistent with our sense of the

platform's focus on low-ticket discretionary verticals. While the platform lags for areas of the shopping experience such as perceived product quality, delivery speeds, and ease of returns. Similarly in Mexico, both Shopee and Temu lag on these attributes — but unlike Brazil, consumers do not credit the platforms with meaningfully above-average views of price perception. As such, despite high purchase frequency for Shopee Brazil and initial traction for Temu Mexico usage in our survey, with a skew to lower-ticket categories we believe surveyed frequency share likely over-indexes versus GMV share for both platforms.

## Engagement Trends — App Data

Shopee led for incremental app downloads in Brazil in '23, but at a decelerating pace; in Mexico, Temu has inflected upward since its market entry, but MELI retains a wide lead. Looking to app download trends as another measure of platforms' consumer reach, we have seen a convergence in accumulated downloads between MELI, Americanas, and Shopee in Brazil ( Exhibit 88 ). For 2023 trends, Shopee added \~38 million downloads (to 181 million accumulated), versus \~41 million added for 2022 overall. Conversely, Americanas added just \~7 million (to 182 million accumulated). While Amazon continues a steady gain cadence, the \~60 million download base is \~1/3rd the size of MELI / Americanas / Shopee. Looking to Mexico ( Exhibit 89 ), MELI has a wide lead at 90 million accumulated downloads, while we note gains for Walmex (to 47 million), in second place; we see Walmex's app momentum as consistent with the incremental traction shown in our AlphaWise survey. We further highlight the Mexico inflection of Temu, having reached 45 million downloads in just its first \~8 months in the market.

Exhibit 88: We see convergence in the accumulated download base between MELI, Americanas, and Shopee in Brazil...

Source: SensorTower, Morgan Stanley Research
Note: Americanas includes Americanas, Submarino and Shoptime; Magazine Luiza includes Magalu,Netshoes, Zattini and Epoca.

## eCommerce Market Share Models

We have increasing conviction around MELI as the LatAm eCommerce share leader, supported by our Power of Marketplaces global analysis. As shown in Exhibit 90 , we continue to see growth headroom in LatAm eCommerce which supports an incremental GMV opportunity for multiple operators. However, we believe MELI is increasingly establishing itself as the dominant platform across the region, supported by its marketplace scale, fintech tie-ins, and logistics/fulfillment presence. Looking at 2023E figures, we see MELI's 34% region-wide share as nearly double that of the next 7 largest operators combined ( Exhibit 91 ). Operations for eCommerce are largely conducted on a country-level basis, but gains from platform scale, development, and data analysis can be spread across the region. For other key competitors not directly tracked in our modeling, we see Shopee remaining as a relevant operator in Brazil, but with a diminished presence elsewhere following the 2022 move to cross-border–only operations in Mexico/Chile/ Colombia and market exit in Argentina. For Amazon, we see a scaled presence in Mexico (estimated #2 operator behind MELI) and incremental traction in Brazil, while the company has kept a measured expansion approach in its 10+ years in the LatAm region. For Temu, the platform entered Mexico in May 2023 and Chile in August; we look for further expansion (including into Brazil), but believe it remains early for a meaningful impact on region-wide market share; see our September 2023 Cross-Border FAQs for additional detail.

Exhibit 90: While we see growth runway in LatAm for multiple eCommerce operators…
LatAm eCommerce Sales by Company (US\$bn)
![](images/de02867a3649d299ea57dd59c06e51d34053b5cb7a92f4af43424b871b5b18d2.jpg)
Source: Euromonitor, Company data, Morgan Stanley Research estimates
Exhibit 91: … on a regional basis MELI has a dominant share position
LatAm eCommerce Share by Company (2023E)

![](images/43dccf21b643e772cfd2b371fff89bf1a718bc76ed77da3d504a07bce2199528.jpg)
Source: Euromonitor, Company data, Morgan Stanley Research estimates

For Brazil-based operators, growth trends diverged through 2023, with MELI leading, Shopee decelerating, and Magalu / Casas Bahia seeing limited pickup despite Americanas' disruption. For Brazil eCommerce market share, the key story in 2023 was the shift away from Americanas amid the company's ongoing restructuring, following accounting inconsistencies announced January 11. As shown in Exhibit 92 , we estimate Americanas' online GMV declined \~70% y/y in 2023, ceding \~10pp of market share. MELI was a top beneficiary, accelerating to an estimated 27% growth for 2023E (from 21% in 2022). While despite the share made available, we saw limited evidence of this being captured by Magazine Luiza (+7% 2023E GMV growth, versus +8% in 2022) or Casas Bahia (-2% in 2023E, vs -13% in 2022); we see lower marketplace mix, and pressure from high interest rates, as among the factors preventing greater share gain. While for Shopee Brazil, the deceleration trend continued through 2023, as the platform shifted away from its heaviest periods of shipping and take rate subsidies. From a market share perspective ( Exhibit 93 ), we see MELI gaining a notable +5pp y/y of Brazil eCommerce market share (to 33%). We note that our analysis does not include Amazon Brazil, given limited available data, while based on survey momentum we believe the platform likely featured as an incremental gainer of share ceded from Americanas as well. Going forward, our forecasts imply growth rates converging across platforms as we move past the peak Americanas disruption impact; while given MELI's marketplace scale leadership and more recent traction in electronics (see our Black Friday quick take), our forecast for only modest incremental share gain could ultimately prove too conservative.

Exhibit 92: Brazil eCommerce growth diverged through 2023, with MELI leading, Shopee decelerating, and Magalu / Casas Bahia seeing limited pickup despite Americanas disruption...

## Online Travel

Surveyed travel data shows a greater propensity for online bookings versus in-store; Google leads for research, while we see a mix of international and LatAm platforms (including Despegar) ranking highly for price comparison and booking-related visits. New to our eCommerce survey in 2023, we asked consumers around their preferences and behaviors when booking leisure / vacation travel. We show the activities done when booking travel, by socio-economic class, for Brazil in Exhibit 94 and Mexico in Exhibit 95 . In both countries, checking prices through online travel agencies was the most-cited activity. While for purchasing tickets, we saw a higher propensity to buy tickets online (33% in Brazil, 36% in Mexico) than at physical / in-store agencies (15% in Brazil, 13% in Mexico). Despite the development of artificial intelligence tools, "get help/ideas from an AI chatbot" was the least-used activity of the items surveyed in both countries. At the platform level, as shown in Exhibit 96 for Brazil and Exhibit 97 for Mexico, Google ranked highest in both countries as the first platform visited for researching travel online. Despegar's Brazil property Decolar.com ranked second, and was in the top tier of companies for visits around comparing prices and for consumers that know the trip they want to book. In Mexico, Despegar's Despegar.com and Best Day properties ranked lower. Among international online travel sites, Trivago ranked highly in both countries, with Booking.com featuring among top platforms in Brazil, and Expedia in Mexico.

## On-Demand Delivery

Looking to on-demand delivery platforms, serving the highest-frequency categories such as ready-to-eat food and groceries, our AlphaWise data shows iFood leading in Brazil, and UberEats and Rappi in Mexico. Within our coverage, we have seen companies continue to shorten delivery times. MELI, for example, has averaged \~55% of volumes delivered same or next-day in 9M23, while this was largely similar to 2022 levels. As we detailed in our 2021 Same-Day Delivery analysis, we built to same-day tapping into underpenetrated eCommerce categories worth \~R\$700 billion, or \~50% of total Brazil retail. When pivoting toward this segment, competition expands from traditional eCommerce players to include on-demand delivery specialists — including private companies iFood and Rappi. On-demand specialists' business models are based on selling third-party products primarily in the restaurant / meal, grocery & household products, and over-thecounter drug categories; these platforms generally fulfill orders directly from stores using motorcycle, car, or bicycle couriers. While lagging behind the largest marketplaces (with \~170 million accumulated downloads for MELI, \~180 million for Shopee), we see substantial user bases including \~130 million for iFood (up \~16 million y/y) and 29 million for Rappi (up \~1 million y/y). Our AlphaWise usage frequency data by platform is shown as Exhibit 98 for Brazil and Exhibit 99 for Mexico. In Brazil, iFood leads, with 80% of surveyed consumers having used the app over the past six months (78% in 2022). Ze Delivery (operated by Ambev / ABEV3.SA, covered by Ricardo Alves) and Rappi (private) were the next highest-ranking platforms. While in Mexico, there was a close top-two between Uber Eats (78% past 6-month usage) and Rappi (73%). For the delivery apps surveyed, ready-to-eat food remains the most-purchased category.

Exhibit 98: iFood retained a wide lead in P6M usage among delivery apps in Brazil...

# Sub-Sector Outlooks

## Brazil Apparel Retail

We see a mixed top-down setup for Brazil apparel, with potential macro tailwinds but a move past the peak re-opening boost; for gross margins, CPI vs PPI trends appear supportive. While volatility remains in areas such as seasonal weather trends, we believe 2023 marked a normalized year for the Brazil apparel segment, following '20/'21 store closures and the pent-up demand rebound in '22 ( Exhibit 100 ). Amid this backdrop, 9M23 comps for Renner averaged -1.8%, with C&A's apparel comps a more favorable +5.8%. Looking into 2024, we believe the apparel segment could be a beneficiary of improved consumption trends amid lower interest rates, and a shift to less restrictive lending standards could benefit apparel retail sales as well. We believe this is reflected in our 2024E comps of +8.8% for Renner, and apparel comps of +6.5% for C&A, while we see limited catalyst for upside convexity versus our forecasts. On the gross margin side, we note a tailwind from lower textile costs, with the producer price index for textiles remaining in negative territory in recent months ( Exhibit 101 ). A favorable CPI versus PPI gap should be a tailwind for the apparel segment, while amid continued industry competition we believe this benefit could be largely re-invested back into product prices.

Exhibit 100:Following peak pandemic and re-opening volatility, we see Brazil apparel having settled into a more normalized top line backdrop…

![](images/504771ffcd8e7a7784118254cbd02ec860214ebb71b1fa0813c57336ef7b1e80.jpg)
Source: IBGE, Company data, Morgan Stanley Research estimates
Exhibit 101:… while for gross margins, we see an input cost tailwind from lower textile producer price growth

![](images/1ee4473d2c1f34294424a2ff915fe77bedc0156a7a979d739a75c4372ffaf6b9.jpg)
Source: IBGE, Morgan Stanley Research

For online operations, our survey data points to a continued rise in competition, including from cross-border platforms; results for Renner and C&A showed a modest downward trend. As we highlighted in Debates for 2H23: Can LatAm Apparel Margins Recover? (31 May 2023), we have maintained caution around operating margin trends for LatAm apparel & department store operators, with online costs and competition driving structural pressure to the industry profit pool versus pre-Covid levels. Results from our AlphaWise eCommerce survey further supports this thesis, in our view. As shown in Exhibit 102 , cross-border platforms (led by Sea Limited's Shopee) lead for online purchase frequency in Brazil apparel. C&A ranked fifth, and Renner eighth among surveyed platforms, with C&A continuing a multiyear downtrend in our survey results, and Renner to a more moderate degree. We forecast '24E retail margins for Renner to remain \~400bps below 2019 levels (at 14.5%, ex-IFRS16) and \~300bps below 2019 levels for C&A (at 9.2%, ex-IFRS16). In Mexico ( Exhibit 103 ), apparel results for Liverpool were steadier, with a number two spot in between Amazon and MELI, while we note the early emergence of Temu with an over-indexing to the apparel vertical.

Exhibit 102:Brazil local apparel operators continue to lag crossborder platforms and MELI for online purchases within the category

Source: AlphaWise, Morgan Stanley Research
Mexico: Clothing, Shoes & Accessories Category Bought in Past 12 Months by Retailer

## Brazil Food Retail

We remain constructive on Brazil Food Retail into 2024, with our Global Discounters analysis reenforcing our conviction around the cash & carry expansion opportunity. As we detailed in Global Food Retail: Discounters: Growth Plus Resilience (30 Nov 2023), we see discount food retail as one of the best-positioned retail formats globally. With a secular trend toward value-seeking, our analysis shows growth through macro cycles, plus resilience from the online threat. Our scorecard analysis showed Brazil as a highlyinsulated market from hard discount competition, which we believe supports cash & carry as the dominant discount format in the country. At \~20% of total grocery retail sales for '22, by our calculation, we forecast the warehouse club segment (including cash & carry) reaching 26% of industry sales by 2027E. Looking to the penetration opportunity, our global comparison analysis shows that there are countries with higher penetration for discount-type stores than Brazil — including Germany at \~36%, Poland \~35%, and Turkey \~30%; this compares to Brazil at \~20% including both warehouse clubs and discounters in 2022 ( Exhibit 104 ). Altogether, we forecast warehouse club sales reaching 26.3% of the market by 2027E (from 19.6% in 2022), while we believe hypermarkets' share will decline from 8.1% in 2022 to 6.0% by 2027E ( Exhibit 105 ).

Exhibit 104:Our global benchmarking analysis suggest further penetration room for warehouse clubs in Brazil, with top global markets at 30%+ discounter penetration, vs. Brazil at \~20%

![](images/d2f247f70dbdac3a4c855487259546c6464a3f3af23f1956429db78a9d3c9309.jpg)
Source: Euromonitor, Local National Sources, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 105:We forecast warehouse clubs (including C&C) gaining \~7pp of share in '22-'27E, reaching 26% of total grocery store sales; hypermarkets' share donation likely continues

![](images/d6330218e42488e659bb0088ca8c962333dc4c845208758aae7a5d856d4b62d5.jpg)

For what's ailed the cash & carry sector over the past year, food deflation has been a meaningful weight, while our analysis points to a potential turn in this cyclical headwind. Notably, we think the trough of this cycle can be relatively shallower than the previous 2017-18 food deflation cycle. For context, the 2017-18 period extended for 12 months, with the trough of the cycle reaching -5% y/y at-home food deflation. Morgan Stanley Brazil economist Thiago Machado forecasts general food inflation (including both at-home and on-premise prices) to have troughed in October '23 ( Exhibit 106 ); if we hold stable on-premise food inflation until the end of 2024, this would imply at-home food deflation extending until June 2024 — for an 11 month deflationary period. Looking to the category breakdown ( Exhibit 107 ), we note potential receding food deflation in meats and potential upward pressures in fruits, grains, flours, and other vegetables, with El Nino and the Indian Ocean Diopole posing a near-term risk to global weather/crops (see the MS El Nino deep dive). Altogether, while we believe at-home food deflation will persist at

least until 1H24, our collective analysis points to a \~12-month cycle, with a potentially shallower trough vs. the 2017-18 period.

Exhibit 106:Our Brazil economics teams sees overall food inflation bottoming in October '23, followed by sequential improvements

Source: IBGE, Morgan Stanley Research

![](images/f435e825708456e655dd9ba849004cacff81217de581dfc3d205d959cedc53d9.jpg)

## LatAm Specialty Retail

For LatAm beauty, we see eCommerce growth potential and direct selling resilience, with online gains largely coming from store-based retail. We continue to see a supportive eCommerce growth backdrop for the LatAm beauty industry, with the category ranking toward the top in both Brazil and Mexico for past-year and expected spend increases. Against this backdrop, our AlphaWise survey data in Exhibit 108 show that beauty pure-plays (led by O Boticario and Natura) are the top online destinations for the category. While Shopee had been on a gaining trajectory, this slowed in our 2022 data and slightly revered in 2023. Looking to the latest-available full-year data from Euromonitor (through 2022), we see that eCommerce continued to gain prevalence, reaching 8.3% of the channel in Brazil ( Exhibit 109 ). While notably, direct sales penetration increased \~10bps, versus a \~160bps decline in 2021. We believe this data point supports a case around direct selling resilience, with online gains coming from the store-based retailing channel.

Exhibit 108:AlphaWise survey data show that pure-play beauty brands lead for category purchases; Shopee remains in the top three, but its momentum in our survey has slowed

Brazil: Beauty and Personal Care Category Bought in Past 12 Months by Retailer

![](images/9c41ddfb0d03cac6925e5eff3d93963bfb1fa21ec51b9f45423238e04dc25258.jpg)

Exhibit 109:The direct sales channel held share in Brazil beauty for 2022, with online gains coming from the store-based retail channel
![](images/64ef95fe4d709650aab262ac0b7d38acd42f22631d70a9f6b039b13a5ee5a152.jpg)
Source: Euromonitor, Morgan Stanley Research
Source: AlphaWise, Morgan Stanley Research

For the pet segment, we see structural tailwinds for the superstore model, and note that pure-plays rank favorably for eCommerce in the category. Our global

benchmarking shows a continued opportunity for the pet superstore segment in Brazil. As shown in Exhibit 110 , the superstore segment represents 12% of the pet care market in Brazil, versus 31% in Germany, and 17% in the US. We believe this larger, more formalized channel has a share gain opportunity from mom-and-pop pet shops, which currently command \~50% share of the Brazil market. At the company level, our most recent AlphaWise survey notably showed Petz ranking at the top for consumers' online purchases in the category, with similar purchase penetration versus 2022 levels ( Exhibit 111 ). Online pure-play Petlove, new to our survey, ranked second, while pet superstore peer Cobasi was fourth. While MercadoLibre ranked third, with increasing penetration y/y, we believe our survey analysis shows pet food / supplies as one of the more insulated retail categories from the eCommerce marketplace threat.

Brazil: Pet Food / Supplies Category Bought in Past 12 Months by Retaile

Exhibit 110:We see a positive setup for pet superstores, as Brazil under-indexes in this channel when compared to Germany and the US

![](images/f8d8bac46dc34a309551f8a37ff6f13df345a9b83a9fec7ced85dd6c26dd5ae8.jpg)
Source: Euromonitor, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 111:Our AlphaWise survey highlights Petz leading for online purchases in the category, followed by online pure-play Petlove

![](images/1ab7ba92efc7146e91e2928e8a9a39a12de24b9e806751ab757537d4dda822a0.jpg)
Source: AlphaWise, Morgan Stanley Research

For the furniture channel, we've continued to see an overhang from pandemic-driven demand pull-forward; pure-plays lag marketplaces in our survey data. The furniture channel was an outsized beneficiary of stay-at-home habits during '20/'21; the segment in Brazil grew +11% y/y (real terms) in 2020, and +5% in 2021. This reversed to a -11% y/y decline in 2022, and -6% through 10M23 ( Exhibit 112 ). The worst could be behind us as interest rates decline, providing a tailwind for cyclical sectors. However, given the highticket nature of furniture purchases, and refresh cycles that can be even longer than consumer electronics, we see limited catalyst for an upward demand inflection into 2024. At the company level, our AlphaWise Survey results ( Exhibit 113 ) show that marketplaces rank higher than pure-plays (including Mobly) for purchase frequency, while we note the vertical surveyed includes both furniture items and smaller accessories (where platforms such as top-ranked Shopee likely over-index).

Exhibit 112:After demand pull-forward for furniture during the pandemic, the segment has reversed to declines, with real sales -6% on average in 10M23

![](images/ccc9646e77fcb9ed4c8e4c301b4647a30a73f6b8ee0f30457bc401ea86f5e4cf.jpg)
Source: IBGE, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 113:AlphaWise data shows marketplace platforms surveying above pure-plays for purchase frequency in the home furnishings and accessories category

![](images/f9c756c132be2c7bb2dce4bed315657462b18f6f912ea5bdaf174bd3470fff8a.jpg)
Source: AlphaWise, Morgan Stanley Research

## LatAm Online Travel

For LatAm online travel, we see a constructive combination of macro tailwinds, normalizing traffic, and greater digital bookings share. Looking to industry data for Brazil ( Exhibit 114 ), we highlight the high correlation between growth in travel spend and growth in GDP. As an additional factor supporting travel demand, we point to the significant disruption the sector faced during peak Covid-19 lockdown periods. While trends have recovered from the bottom, we continue to see pent-up demand. Looking to airport traffic data ( Exhibit 115 ), we see a recovery trend, with Mexico surpassing prepandemic levels more quickly than Argentina and Brazil. Further, we continue to see room for digitalization trends to power greater online share in the travel industry — forecast at 52% of volumes for 2027E, vs. 45% in 2022. Travel bookings in LatAm have continued along the digitalization curve, supported by drivers such as the proliferation of mobile internet and an expansion in ways to pay online. Looking to travel bookings across the region ( Exhibit 116 ), online transactions represented 45% of total industry volumes in 2022, up +10pp from 35% in 2017. During the 2017-22 period, the online travel industry posted near-zero cumulative growth in USD terms, on the combination of pandemic disruption effects and FX depreciation. Coupling continued penetration gains with a more favorable top-down backdrop, we look for the sector to resume a sustainable growth trend going forward. For further industry detail, see our August 2023 Despegar Assumption of Coverage report.

Exhibit 114:Looking at travel industry data, we see a strong relationship between y/y travel spending and GDP growth (real terms) in Brazil

![](images/29f46572caecb461675d418a798830e61a30d86a839087fbdf5642e1187dee36.jpg)
Source: IBGE, Euromonitor, Morgan Stanley Research
